Edge features update and grading edge with SoftMax

In updating edge features, we observe that in graph neural networks, edges serve as the carriers of message passing, and their properties are primarily determined by the nodes they connect. Furthermore, within the designed task framework, there exists a mutual exclusion relationship among some of the edges connected to the same node. Based on this observation, we employ softMax to score the edges and utilize these scores for updating edge features. This section primarily elaborates on the design rationale and provides a mathematical explanation. Readers who are not seeking detailed derivations may skip this part.

Similarly, if an edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{u_{x}u_{y}}$$\end{document}euxuy belongs to the graph path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left[v_{u_{1}},e_{u_{1}u_{2}},v_{u_{2}},e_{u_{2}u_{3}},v_{u_{1}},\ldots ,v_{u_{x}},e_{u_{x}u_{y}},v_{u_{y}},\ldots ,v_{u_{m}} \right]$$\end{document}vu1,eu1u2,vu2,eu2u3,vu1,…,vux,euxuy,vuy,…,vum corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu, then the triplet \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(v_{u_{x}},e_{u_{x}u_{y}},v_{u_{y}})$$\end{document}(vux,euxuy,vuy) composed of the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{u_{x}u_{y}}$$\end{document}euxuy and its two endpoint nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{u_{x}}$$\end{document}vux and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{u_{y}}$$\end{document}vuy should possess a motion pattern. Furthermore, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{u_{x}u_{y}}$$\end{document}euxuy belongs to the graph path corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{u_{x}}$$\end{document}vux and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{u_{y}}$$\end{document}vuy should exchange messages through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{u_{x}u_{y}}$$\end{document}euxuy to bring their respective features \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{u_{x}}^{'}$$\end{document}pux′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{u_{y}}^{'}$$\end{document}puy′ closer to the feature vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{u}^{'}$$\end{document}pu′ corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu.

Then, a straightforward approach would be to determine the feature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{xy}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}qxyn+1 of the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy}$$\end{document}exy based on the neighbor node features \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{x}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}pxn+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{y}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}pyn+1 after the message passing process, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{xy}^{n + 1} = h(p_{x}^{n + 1},p_{y}^{n + 1},\theta _{4})$$\end{document}qxyn+1=h(pxn+1,pyn+1,θ4). However, if our goal is to achieve the targeted and specific message passing mentioned earlier, this simple approach falls short as it treats the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy}$$\end{document}exy as an appendage to the nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{x}$$\end{document}vx and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{y}$$\end{document}vy, ignoring the relationships between edges in the graph structure.

Given the n-partite structure of graph G and the absence of nodes belonging to the same part in the graph path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left[v_{u_{1}},e_{u_{1}u_{2}},v_{u_{2}},e_{u_{2}u_{3}},v_{u_{1}},\ldots ,v_{u_{x}},e_{u_{x}u_{y}},v_{u_{y}},\ldots ,v_{u_{m}} \right]$$\end{document}vu1,eu1u2,vu2,eu2u3,vu1,…,vux,euxuy,vuy,…,vum corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu, there exist numerous mutual exclusion relationships among the edges incident to a given node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{x}$$\end{document}vx, such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy_{1}},e_{xy_{2}},\ldots ,e_{xy_{g}},\ldots ,e_{xy_{h}}$$\end{document}exy1,exy2,…,exyg,…,exyh. For instance, if nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{y_{g}^{1}}$$\end{document}vyg1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{y_{g}^{2}}$$\end{document}vyg2, both neighbors of node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{x}$$\end{document}vx, belong to the same part, then the edges connecting them, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy_{g}^{1}}$$\end{document}exyg1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy_{g}^{2}}$$\end{document}exyg2, exhibit a mutual exclusion relationship.

Assuming that the node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{u_{x}}$$\end{document}vux belonging to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu has neighboring nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{u_{y}},v_{y_{g}^{1}},v_{y_{g}^{2}},\ldots ,v_{y_{g}^{n}}$$\end{document}vuy,vyg1,vyg2,…,vygn from the same part, where only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{u_{y}}$$\end{document}vuy belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu, we cannot distinguish based on the available information whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{u_{y}},v_{y_{g}^{1}},v_{y_{g}^{2}},\ldots ,v_{y_{g}^{n}}$$\end{document}vuy,vyg1,vyg2,…,vygn belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu. Since we know that only one of them belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu, it follows that only one of the edges connecting them belongs to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu. This leads to the constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p( T_{u}|e_{u_{x}u_{y}} ) + p( T_{u}|e_{u_{x}y_{g}^{1}} ) + p( T_{u}|e_{u_{x}y_{g}^{2}} ) + \ldots + p( T_{u}|e_{u_{x}y_{g}^{n}} ) \le 1$$\end{document}p(Tu|euxuy)+p(Tu|euxyg1)+p(Tu|euxyg2)+…+p(Tu|euxygn)≤1.

We implement the constraint and the probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p( T_{u}{|e}_{xy} )$$\end{document}p(Tu|exy) using SoftMax and a scoring function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l(x,\theta _{2})$$\end{document}l(x,θ2), respectively. Consequently, the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy}$$\end{document}exy receives two weights, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${gra}_{xy}^{1}$$\end{document}graxy1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${gra}_{yx}^{1}$$\end{document}grayx1, which we refer to as “message passing strengths”. Based on these weights and the updated node features \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{x}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}pxn+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{y}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}pyn+1 after message passing, we update the feature of edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy}$$\end{document}exy as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{xy}^{n + 1} = h(( p_{x}^{n + 1},{gra}_{xy}^{1}{,p}_{y}^{n + 1},{gra}_{yx}^{1},q_{xy}^{n} ),\theta _{4})$$\end{document}qxyn+1=h((pxn+1,graxy1,pyn+1,grayx1,qxyn),θ4).

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy} \in T_{u}$$\end{document}exy∈Tu, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{x}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}pxn+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{y}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}pyn+1 provide specific information about \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu to the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy}$$\end{document}exy, and through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x,\theta _{4})$$\end{document}h(x,θ4), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{xy}^{'}$$\end{document}qxy′ is steered towards \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{n}^{'}$$\end{document}qn′. Conversely, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy}$$\end{document}exy does not belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x,\theta _{4})$$\end{document}h(x,θ4) uses the tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( p_{x}^{n + 1},{gra}_{xy}^{1}{,p}_{y}^{n + 1},{gra}_{yx}^{1},q_{xy}^{n} )$$\end{document}(pxn+1,graxy1,pyn+1,grayx1,qxyn) to determine that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy} \notin T_{u}$$\end{document}exy∉Tu and steers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{xy}^{'}$$\end{document}qxy′ away from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{n}^{'}$$\end{document}qn′.

Alternatively, if a significant amount of “message passing” occurs between nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{x}$$\end{document}vx and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{y}$$\end{document}vy through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy}$$\end{document}exy, resulting in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{x}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}pxn+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{y}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}pyn+1, the simplest approach is to record this information in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{xy}^{n + 1}$$\end{document}qxyn+1. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x,\theta _{4})$$\end{document}h(x,θ4) needs to utilize the tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( p_{x}^{n + 1},{gra}_{xy}^{1}{,p}_{y}^{n + 1},{gra}_{yx}^{1},q_{xy}^{n} )$$\end{document}(pxn+1,graxy1,pyn+1,grayx1,qxyn) to make the necessary judgments and maintain an accurate “record”. Figure 14 illustrates that our algorithm gradually distinguish different classes of edge through process above.Fig. 14The PCA clustering results of edge features at various levels indicate that, as message passing and feature updating progress, the differences in features between positive samples (orange) and negative samples (blue) become increasingly pronounced.

The PCA clustering results of edge features at various levels indicate that, as message passing and feature updating progress, the differences in features between positive samples (orange) and negative samples (blue) become increasingly pronounced.

Training method and loss function

According to the graph tasks we have previously defined, the multi-layer perceptron, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{1}$$\end{document}w1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{2}$$\end{document}w2, perform supervised learning on the outputs p(v) and p(e) based on the final features \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p^{'}$$\end{document}p′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q^{'}$$\end{document}q′ of nodes v and edges e, respectively, comparing them with the pre-defined labels \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{GT}(v)$$\end{document}pGT(v) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{GT}(e)$$\end{document}pGT(e). However, during the training process, the large number of false targets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_i^t$$\end{document}oit in the original video often results in a large-scale graph G. Taking the scenario of Image Window=8 as an example, a single graph in the Ground Truth dataset for training may contain up to hundreds of nodes and tens of thousands of edges, leading to significant resource consumption that precludes training on most GPUs.

Drawing from our discussions in Sections 3.5.3 and 3.5.4, message passing in the GMP model is typically targeted, with negative samples or messages passed between nodes belonging to different categories often being less significant. Therefore, we can randomly sample negative samples or nodes from other categories while preserving positive samples or a specific category of nodes48. A large-scale graph G can be sampled to generate multiple small-scale graphs, and combined with randomization and the setting of hyperparameters such as batch size during training, the training process becomes more stable, often leading to more ideal results. Additionally, we can re-sample the graph G between training epochs to obtain different outcomes, effectively implementing a form of data augmentation that can effectively mitigate overfitting in the model. An example of sampling is shown in Figure 15.Fig. 15Schematic Diagram of the Graph Sampling Process. Red represents the Positive Sample while Blue represents Negative one. (Note: We Retain All Positive Samples and Only Sample on Negative Samples).

Schematic Diagram of the Graph Sampling Process. Red represents the Positive Sample while Blue represents Negative one. (Note: We Retain All Positive Samples and Only Sample on Negative Samples).

Interestingly, our graph neural network does not consume excessive memory during the inference mode due to the elimination of the need to store gradients, computation graphs, and intermediate results. Consequently, the algorithm does not require sampling the graph G during inference.

In terms of loss function selection, we have chosen the cross-entropy loss function. For the graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G\left\{ V,E \right\}$$\end{document}GV,E, we compute the average loss across all nodes and edges and then directly sum them up:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & loss = - ~\frac{1}{V(G)}{\sum {\left. \left( p \right. _{GT}\left( v_{x} \right) { \log \left( {p\left( v_{x} \right) } \right) } + \left( 1 - p \right. _{GT}\left( v_{x} \right) \right) log\left( 1 - p\left( v_{x} \right) \right) )}} \\ & + \frac{1}{E(G)}{\sum {\left. \left( p \right. _{GT}\left( e_{xy} \right) { \log \left( {p\left( e_{xy} \right) } \right) } + \left( 1 - p \right. _{GT}\left( e_{xy} \right) \right) log\left( 1 - p\left( e_{xy} \right) \right) )~}} \\ & where~v_{x} \in V,~e_{xy} \in E;~V,E \in G\left\{ V,E \right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}loss=-1V(G)∑pGTvxlogpvx+1-pGTvxlog1-pvx)+1E(G)∑pGTexylogpexy+1-pGTexylog1-pexy)wherevx∈V,exy∈E;V,E∈GV,ENow let us revisit the cross-entropy loss function, which is expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l = - \sum {ylogx + (1 - y)log(1 - x)}$$\end{document}l=-∑ylogx+(1-y)log(1-x) where y represents the label of the sample and x is the output result. Assuming in practical scenarios that the average correct prediction rate for positive samples is p and for negative samples is q, with the respective numbers of positive and negative samples being m and n, the total loss can be expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l = - \frac{1}{m + n}(mlogp + n\log q)$$\end{document}l=-1m+n(mlogp+nlogq).

We can calculate the derivatives of the loss function with respect to p and q as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\partial l}{\partial p} = - \frac{1}{p}\frac{m}{m + n},\frac{\partial l}{\partial q} = - \frac{1}{q}\frac{n}{m + n}$$\end{document}∂l∂p=-1pmm+n,∂l∂q=-1qnm+n. Since p and q are generated by the Sigmoid function, the gradients passed back to the classifier are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\partial l}{\partial w} = \frac{\partial l}{\partial p}\frac{\partial p}{\partial w} + \frac{\partial l}{\partial q}\frac{\partial q}{\partial w} = - \frac{m}{m + n}\frac{1}{p}p(1 - p) - \frac{n}{m + n}\frac{1}{q}q(1 - q) = - \frac{1}{m + n}(m(1 - p) + n(1 - q))$$\end{document}∂l∂w=∂l∂p∂p∂w+∂l∂q∂q∂w=-mm+n1pp(1-p)-nm+n1qq(1-q)=-1m+n(m(1-p)+n(1-q)). Here, the gradient ratio contributed by p and q, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\nabla p}{\nabla q}$$\end{document}∇p∇q, is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{m(1 - p)}{n(1 - q)}$$\end{document}m(1-p)n(1-q).

Furthermore, we can compute precision (pre) and recall as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$pre = \frac{\frac{m}{m + n}p}{\frac{n}{m + n}(1 - q) + \frac{m}{m + n}p} = \frac{pm}{pm + (1 - q)n}$$\end{document}pre=mm+npnm+n(1-q)+mm+np=pmpm+(1-q)n and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$recall = \frac{mp}{m} = p$$\end{document}recall=mpm=p. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$pre = recall$$\end{document}pre=recall, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{m(1 - p)}{n(1 - q)} = 1$$\end{document}m(1-p)n(1-q)=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla p = \nabla q$$\end{document}∇p=∇q, and the model evenly improves the performance of both positive and negative samples. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$pre> recall$$\end{document}pre>recall, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{m(1 - p)}{n(1 - q)} < 1$$\end{document}m(1-p)n(1-q)<1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla p < \nabla q$$\end{document}∇p<∇q, and the model tends to improve the performance of negative samples more, emphasizing recall. Conversely, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$pre < recall$$\end{document}pre<recall, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{m(1 - p)}{n(1 - q)}> 1$$\end{document}m(1-p)n(1-q)>1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla p> \nabla q$$\end{document}∇p>∇q, and the model favors improving the performance of positive samples, emphasizing pre.

The original cross-entropy function aims to achieve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$pre = recall$$\end{document}pre=recall. However, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \ll n$$\end{document}m≪n, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 - p \gg 1 - q$$\end{document}1-p≫1-q, meaning the model tends to improve q to a higher level before improving p. This can result in a low precision pre for a significant portion of the training process, and positive samples may not receive sufficient attention.

To address this, we consider a weighted loss function: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l = - \sum {a_{1}y\log x + a_{2}(1 - y)log(1 - x)}$$\end{document}l=-∑a1ylogx+a2(1-y)log(1-x) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{1}$$\end{document}a1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{2}$$\end{document}a2 are weighting coefficients. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{1} = a_{2} = 1$$\end{document}a1=a2=1, it becomes the original cross-entropy function. Setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{1}> a_{2}$$\end{document}a1>a2 allows the model to focus more on positive samples, emphasizing the improvement of p and recall. Conversely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{2}> a_{1}$$\end{document}a2>a1 directs the model’s attention to negative samples, emphasizing the enhancement of q and pre.

At the initial stage of our model training, we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{1}> a_{2}$$\end{document}a1>a2. As the loss function decreases and p and q improve, we gradually decrease \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{1}$$\end{document}a1 until \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{1} \approx a_{2} \approx 1$$\end{document}a1≈a2≈1. In practice, we use the following expressions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{1}$$\end{document}a1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{2}$$\end{document}a2: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{1} = \frac{m + n}{2Max((loss + 0.15)\sqrt{mn} + ( - loss + 0.85)(\frac{m + n}{2}),\sqrt{mn})}$$\end{document}a1=m+n2Max((loss+0.15)mn+(-loss+0.85)(m+n2),mn) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{2} = \frac{m + n}{2(m + n - Max((loss + 0.15)\sqrt{mn} + ( - loss + 0.85)(\frac{m + n}{2}),\sqrt{mn}))}$$\end{document}a2=m+n2(m+n-Max((loss+0.15)mn+(-loss+0.85)(m+n2),mn)), In this expression, loss refers to the value of loss function from the previous epoch, and the initial loss can be considered as positive infinity or a large number.

Organize and output results

In Section 3.4, we constructed several graphs using the potential targets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_{i}^{j}$$\end{document}oij from the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${[O}^{1},O^{2},\ldots ,O^{t},\ldots ,O^{n}]$$\end{document}[O1,O2,…,Ot,…,On]. For each probability graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^{p}$$\end{document}Gp, we obtained the probabilities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(v_{x})$$\end{document}p(vx) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(e_{xy})$$\end{document}p(exy) that each node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{x}$$\end{document}vx and edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{xy}$$\end{document}exy belongs to a particular trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu in the graph G. These probabilities correspond to the likelihood of a potential target \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}}$$\end{document}oixx belonging to a trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}})$$\end{document}p(oixx), and the probability of two potential targets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}}$$\end{document}oixx and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}}$$\end{document}oiyy belonging to the same trajectory, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}},o_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}})$$\end{document}q(oixx,oiyy). It is worth noting that during the process of graph construction based on Image Window and Gap, some targets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}}$$\end{document}oixx and connections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}},o_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}})$$\end{document}(oixx,oiyy) may appear in multiple probability graphs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^{p}$$\end{document}Gp. In such cases, we simply select the maximum values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}})$$\end{document}p(oixx) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}},o_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}})$$\end{document}q(oixx,oiyy) as the output of the Discriminator.

Subsequently, we utilize the output results \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}})$$\end{document}p(oixx) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}},o_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}})$$\end{document}q(oixx,oiyy) to derive the resulting trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{1},T_{2},\ldots ,T_{s}$$\end{document}T1,T2,…,Ts. The specific process is outlined as follows:

Step 1. Set threshold values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min }$$\end{document}pmin and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{\min }$$\end{document}qmin for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}})$$\end{document}p(oixx) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q(o_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}},o_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}})$$\end{document}q(oixx,oiyy), respectively (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{min},q_{min}> 0$$\end{document}pmin,qmin>0). The set T is composed of all such trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{x}$$\end{document}Tx, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T = \{{T_{1},T_{2},\ldots ,T}_{x},\ldots ,T_{y},\ldots \}$$\end{document}T={T1,T2,…,Tx,…,Ty,…}. Step 4. Identify the two most suitable trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Step 5. Evaluation and results

To train a useful model and validate effectiveness of the algorithm in a real-world, it is imperative to collect table tennis match videos that closely resemble the intended usage scenarios. These videos must be annotated according to the requirements of tactical analysis, serving as the dataset for algorithm training and evaluation. Additionally, to verify the algorithm’s effectiveness and advancement, a comparison with similar solutions is necessary. Such comparisons can also assist in identifying any issues within the algorithm.

Datasets

We have augmented the67 dataset with a significant number of videos, resulting in a comprehensive collection encompassing 20 independent videos as the dataset. Each video is a continuous recording under a fixed scenario, totaling 13,950 frames and 6,027 annotated table tennis balls. In selecting and annotating the videos, we took into account their diversity: our dataset includes scenarios such as competition, training, and warm-up; players ranging from amateurs, student athletes, to professional athletes; and various camera perspectives including side-facing (viewing the table tennis table from the side), directly-facing (viewing the table tennis table from the back), and player-facing (viewing the player directly). In subsequent text, we will refer to the side-facing, directly-facing, and player-facing perspectives as Side-Facing, Directly-Facing, and Player-Facing, respectively. Scenes captured from different perspectives are illustrated in Figure 16. Over 45% of the dataset comes from videos recorded on mobile phones, while more than 30% originates from competition replays sourced from online and televised broadcasts on platforms such as Migu video and CCTV. Additionally, we have appropriately referenced some open-source datasets59 to provide horizontal comparisons and references. An example frame from the dataset is shown in Figure 17, and the specific details of the videos in the dataset are summarized in Table A1 of Appendix A.Fig. 16Illustrations of Images from Different Perspectives: Side-Facing (Left), Directly-Facing (Middle), Player-Facing (Right).Fig. 17Original Video Images from the Dataset.

Illustrations of Images from Different Perspectives: Side-Facing (Left), Directly-Facing (Middle), Player-Facing (Right).

Original Video Images from the Dataset.

Results and comparison

Training and results

We selected the first 17 out of the 20 videos from the dataset for training and testing purposes, while reserving the last 3 videos for validation. It is worth noting that while the last 3 videos share similar scenarios, only one of the 17 videos used for training and testing exhibits a comparable setting. This approach was adopted because the perspectives and scenarios of the last 3 videos are the most popular among current table tennis broadcasts and tactical analyses, and are also the most likely scenarios for the application of our algorithm. Given the diversity of video scenarios and the inconsistency in the number of video frames and table tennis balls captured from different perspectives, we took extra care in dividing the training and testing sets to minimize the impact of data imbalance. For some videos, we chose the first 70% of consecutive frames as the training set, while for others, we selected the first 50%. The remaining 50% of consecutive frames from each video were designated as the testing set. (Specific ranges are detailed in Table A2. of Appendix A.)

During training, we employed the sampling scheme mentioned in Section 3.5.6 to sample the graph G constructed from the training set, while no sampling was performed on the testing set. The final counts of nodes v and edges e in the entire training set are presented in Table 2.Table 2Number of Nodes v and Edges e in the Test Set and Training Set.AttributePositive NumNegative NumTotal NumEdge in Train Set41,7505,278,6995,320,449Node in Train Set14,694190,358205,052Edge in Test Set15,45513,048,64613,064,101Node in Test Set5,572233,227238,799

For training, we set the learning rates for the three-layer message-passing graph neural network GMP and the classifiers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{1}$$\end{document}w1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{2}$$\end{document}w2 to 0.005 and 0.002, respectively. In analyzing the results, we simply used a threshold of 0.5 to categorize nodes v and edges e into positive and negative samples. The performance criteria are precision, recall, and F1-score, which are calculated by TP/(TP + FP), TP/(TP + FN), and 2TP/(2TP + FN + FP), respectively. Here, TP, FP, and FN stand for the number of true positives, false positives, and false negatives, respectively.

The variation of the loss function (Section 3.5.5) and classification accuracy during the entire training process are depicted in Figure 18. Training was conducted using two NVIDIA Tesla-T4 GPUs. After sampling the training set, each epoch comprised approximately 1000 steps, resulting in rapid model convergence. The optimal model was obtained at epoch=8. Additionally, we re-sampled the training set every two training rounds, as described in Section 3.5.6. The initial learning rate for the GMP component was set to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5 \times 10^{- 4}$$\end{document}5×10-4 with a minimum decay of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \times 10^{- 4}$$\end{document}2×10-4, while the initial learning rates for the classifiers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{1}$$\end{document}w1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{2}$$\end{document}w2 were set to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \times 10^{- 4}$$\end{document}2×10-4 with a minimum decay of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \times 10^{- 4}$$\end{document}1×10-4.

Experimental results demonstrate the stability of our training approach, with good performance after model convergence. Only minor overfitting and jittering phenomena were observed.Fig. 18The variation curves of loss function and model performance during training.

The variation curves of loss function and model performance during training.

Recalling to the problem definition in Section 3.1, we need to compare the differences between the set of trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ T_{1},T_{2},\ldots ,T_{s}\}$$\end{document}{T1,T2,…,Ts} output by the algorithm and the Ground Truth set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{1}^{GT},T_{2}^{GT},\ldots ,T_{s}^{GT}$$\end{document}T1GT,T2GT,…,TsGT. For the i-th table tennis ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i}^{t}$$\end{document}bit in the t-th frame belonging to the Ground Truth set, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i}^{t}$$\end{document}bit is also present in the output trajectory set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ T_{1},T_{2},\ldots ,T_{s}$$\end{document}{T1,T2,…,Ts, it will be labeled as a true positive sample. Conversely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i}^{t}$$\end{document}bit will be labeled as a false negative sample if it is not in the output set. Similarly, any sample belonging to the output trajectory set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ T_{1},T_{2},\ldots ,T_{s}\}$$\end{document}{T1,T2,…,Ts} but not to the Ground Truth set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{1}^{GT},T_{2}^{GT},\ldots ,T_{s}^{GT}$$\end{document}T1GT,T2GT,…,TsGT will be labeled as a false positive sample. We will calculate the precision, recall, and F1-score for table tennis ball targets (Target) based on the number of these samples.

Apart from analyzing the table tennis balls \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i}^{t}$$\end{document}bit, we also need to assess the trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu. Consider the Ground Truth trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}^{GT} = \left[b_{i_{1}^{GT}}^{j_{1}^{GT}},b_{i_{2}^{GT}}^{j_{2}^{GT}},\ldots ,b_{i_{m}^{GT}}^{j_{m}^{GT}} \right]$$\end{document}TuGT=bi1GTj1GT,bi2GTj2GT,…,bimGTjmGT, where all adjacent table tennis balls \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(b_{i_{x}^{GT}}^{j_{x}^{GT}},b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}})$$\end{document}(bixGTjxGT,biyGTjyGT) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\ j_{1}^{GT}< j_{2}^{GT}< \ldots< j_{x}^{GT}< j_{y}^{GT}< \ldots < j_{m}^{GT})$$\end{document}(j1GT<j2GT<…<jxGT<jyGT<…<jmGT) are identified. In total, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m - 1$$\end{document}m-1 pairs of adjacent table tennis balls \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(b_{i_{x}^{GT}}^{j_{x}^{GT}},b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}})$$\end{document}(bixGTjxGT,biyGTjyGT) can be extracted from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}^{GT}$$\end{document}TuGT, denoted as the relationship \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(b_{i_{x}^{GT}}^{j_{x}^{GT}},b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}})$$\end{document}R(bixGTjxGT,biyGTjyGT). Next, we investigate whether the pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(b_{i_{x}^{GT}}^{j_{x}^{GT}},b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}})$$\end{document}(bixGTjxGT,biyGTjyGT) belongs to the same trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu within the output trajectory set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ T_{1},T_{2},\ldots ,T_{s}\}$$\end{document}{T1,T2,…,Ts}. If the pair is indeed present in the same trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tu, it is labeled as a true positive sample. Otherwise, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(b_{i_{x}^{GT}}^{j_{x}^{GT}},b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}})$$\end{document}R(bixGTjxGT,biyGTjyGT) is labeled as a false negative sample.

Finally, we examine all relationships \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(b_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}},b_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}})$$\end{document}R(bixx,biyy) within the output trajectory set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ T_{1},T_{2},\ldots ,T_{s}\}$$\end{document}{T1,T2,…,Ts}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}}$$\end{document}bixx and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}}$$\end{document}biyy do not belong to the same trajectory in the Ground Truth set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ T_{1}^{GT},T_{2}^{GT},\ldots ,T_{s}^{GT} \}$$\end{document}{T1GT,T2GT,…,TsGT}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(b_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}},b_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}})$$\end{document}R(bixx,biyy) is labeled as a false positive sample. Similarly, we calculate the precision, recall, and F1-score for all relationship groups \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(b_{i_{x}}^{j_{x}},b_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}})$$\end{document}R(bixx,biyy) (Relationship) based on the sample counts. This analysis provides insights into the effectiveness of the algorithm in reconstructing table tennis ball trajectories.

Based on the content of Section 3.6, the manually set thresholds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min }$$\end{document}pmin and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{\min }$$\end{document}qmin can influence the final test results. To assess this impact, we varied \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min }$$\end{document}pmin within the range of [0.05, 0.7] with a step size of 0.05. For each setting of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min }$$\end{document}pmin, we ran the algorithm with three different values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{\min }$$\end{document}qmin: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min }$$\end{document}pmin, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min } - 0.05$$\end{document}pmin-0.05, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min } + 0.05$$\end{document}pmin+0.05. By averaging the output results, we obtained the mean average precision , mean average recall, and mean average F1-score.

Furthermore, we calculated the average values of these metrics across different threshold ranges. When annotating the results, we used parentheses to indicate the threshold ranges, such as (0.40–0.60) and (0.30–0.70). These ranges represent the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min }$$\end{document}pmin values tested within the specified intervals with a step size of 0.05, and the results were averaged accordingly.

The final results on our dataset are presented in Table 3. The data indicate that our proposed algorithm is not highly sensitive to the manually set thresholds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\min }$$\end{document}pmin and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{\min }$$\end{document}qmin, and it achieves good performance on most datasets.

Compare with other methods

To validate the performance and advancement of our algorithm, we examined other object tracking algorithms and potential alternative solutions, and compared them one by one. The results indicated that our algorithm excels in overall performance.

Comparison with Directly Applying Object Detection Algorithms (YOLOv8)12: We filtered out all images containing table tennis balls from the training and testing sets, and converted the annotated table tennis ball trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\{ T}_{1}^{GT},T_{2}^{GT},\ldots ,T_{s}^{GT}\}$$\end{document}{T1GT,T2GT,…,TsGT} into the dataset format required by YOLOv8. Subsequently, we divided the YOLOv8 dataset into training and testing sets in the ratio of 85% and 15%, respectively. We then trained the default YOLOv8n model for 100 epochs to obtain the best-performing model. In the data evaluation, if the confidence score of a candidate bounding box inferred by the YOLOv8 model exceeded a certain threshold (conf > threshold) and its IOU(Intersection over Union) with any original annotation box of a table tennis ball in the dataset was greater than 0.4, the target was labeled as True Positive. Conversely, it was labeled as False Negative. All candidate boxes with confidence scores above the threshold (conf > threshold) but with IOU values less than 0.4 with all original annotation boxes of table tennis balls in the dataset were labeled as False Positive.

Comparison with Tracking Algorithms: SOT (Step Mode)13: In SOT algorithms, target tracking in videos relies on manual annotations in the first frame. However, the videos in our dataset contain numerous segments without table tennis balls, making it inappropriate to directly apply the SOT algorithm to accurately assess its tracking capability. Therefore, we disassemble the trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}^{GT} = \left[b_{i_{1}^{GT}}^{j_{1}^{GT}},b_{i_{2}^{GT}}^{j_{2}^{GT}},\ldots ,b_{i_{m}^{GT}}^{j_{m}^{GT}} \right]$$\end{document}TuGT=bi1GTj1GT,bi2GTj2GT,…,bimGTjmGT in the dataset into relationships \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(b_{i_{x}^{GT}}^{j_{x}^{GT}},b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}})$$\end{document}R(bixGTjxGT,biyGTjyGT) between adjacent table tennis balls. This approach allows us to evaluate the algorithm’s ability to detect a table tennis ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i_{x}^{GT}}^{j_{x}^{GT}}$$\end{document}bixGTjxGT when another ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}}$$\end{document}biyGTjyGT has already been annotated.

Given that the algorithm uses ResNet as its backbone network, resulting in relatively coarse image granularity, we define a relationship \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(b_{i_{x}^{GT}}^{j_{x}^{GT}},b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}})$$\end{document}R(bixGTjxGT,biyGTjyGT) as True Positive if the bounding box of a detected table tennis ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}}$$\end{document}biyy with a confidence score above a threshold has an IOU(Intersection over Union) greater than 0.3 with the annotated bounding box of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i_{y}^{GT}}^{j_{y}^{GT}}$$\end{document}biyGTjyGT. Conversely, the relationship is labeled as False Positive if the IOU is below this threshold. If no table tennis ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_{i_{y}}^{j_{y}}$$\end{document}biyy is detected, the relationship is marked as False Negative. It is worth noting that we did not retrain the SOT model and instead used the published open-source model directly.

Comparison with Direct Use of Graph Attention Networks (GAT): As an alternative to our GMP message-passing network, we selected a three-layer GAT model for comparison and trained and evaluated it using the same approach as described in Section 4.2.1. It is noteworthy that, due to the slightly faster computation speed of the GAT model compared to our GMP model, we allocated more feature dimensions to the GAT model. Specifically, the input node feature dimensions for each layer of the three-layer GMP model were 70, 96, and 128, respectively. In contrast, the input node feature dimensions for the three-layer GAT model were 438, 502, and 566, respectively.

Furthermore, we compared the performance of the T-FORT algorithm with the FMO algorithm on a subset of videos from the dataset. We compiled statistics on the metrics of these videos and included them in the tables for reference.
