Analysis

Experimental Factors and Error Sample Analysis: Reviewing the hypothesis proposed in Section 3.2, our discriminator is effectively regressing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log \left( p\left( T_{u} \right) \right)$$\end{document}logpTu, making decisions on potential table tennis balls in videos based on both image and motion features. In practical experiments, we observed significant differences in the trajectories and visual morphologies of table tennis balls captured from different camera angles, which are clearly reflected in our experimental data. Taking GMP(0.4–0.6) as an example, we analyzed the average results from three camera angles: Side-Facing, Player-Facing, and Directly-Facing, as summarized in Table 5.

In the Player-Facing view, the table tennis balls appear large in size, with distinct image features and significant contrast against the background due to the proximity to the table tennis table, making them easier to track. In the Side-Facing view, the movement of the table tennis balls exhibits strong regularity (primarily lateral movement). Although the balls may appear small and their image features blurry in some videos, the algorithm still achieves good tracking performance. However, in the Directly-Facing view, the trajectory of the table tennis balls is irregular, and due to interference and occlusion from the players, the balls have low contrast with the background noise, making them the most challenging to track. Consequently, the algorithm performs the worst in this view. The table tennis ball samples successfully detected from the three perspectives of Directly-Facing, Player-Facing, and Side-Facing are shown in Figure 19, 20, and 21, respectively.Table 5Tracking Performance under Different Views.Algorithm NameTargetRelationshipPrecisionRecallF1-ScorePrecisionRecallF1-ScoreSide-Facing0.8620.9540.9060.8530.9410.895Player-Facing0.9500.9530.9520.9300.9150.922Directly-Facing0.8620.8660.8640.8270.8370.832Total0.8800.9460.9120.8650.9270.895

Table 5Tracking Performance under Different Views.Algorithm NameTargetRelationshipPrecisionRecallF1-ScorePrecisionRecallF1-ScoreSide-Facing0.8620.9540.9060.8530.9410.895Player-Facing0.9500.9530.9520.9300.9150.922Directly-Facing0.8620.8660.8640.8270.8370.832Total0.8800.9460.9120.8650.9270.895

Tracking Performance under Different Views.

Analysis of False Negative Targets: The algorithm simply utilizes the HOG image operator, focusing solely on local image features of candidate targets without considering more peripheral or even global image features. The lack of semantic image features results in some table tennis balls that are not distinct from the background or are interfered with by the players’ movements lacking the necessary features for detection, leading to them being missed by the algorithm. Additionally, due to the limitations of the differential detection algorithm, some table tennis balls fail to be accurately outlined with candidate boxes, resulting in the calculation of image features deviating from normal values and causing the subsequent algorithm to overlook these balls. Examples of table tennis balls falsely labeled as negative is shown Figure 22 .Fig. 19Successfully detected table tennis balls in Directly-Facing view (marked with green boxes).Fig. 20Successfully detected table tennis balls in Player-Facing view (marked with green boxes).Fig. 21Successfully detected table tennis balls in Side-Facing view (marked with green boxes).Fig. 22Some examples of table tennis balls falsely labeled as negative (marked with blue boxes).

Successfully detected table tennis balls in Directly-Facing view (marked with green boxes).

Successfully detected table tennis balls in Player-Facing view (marked with green boxes).

Successfully detected table tennis balls in Side-Facing view (marked with green boxes).

Some examples of table tennis balls falsely labeled as negative (marked with blue boxes).

Analysis of False Positive Targets: These targets primarily arise from background noise, local image features and motion trajectories generated by athletes’ movements that closely resemble table tennis balls. Relying solely on local image and motion features of the table tennis balls makes it difficult to discern them accurately. Utilizing sufficiently discriminated data and image features with deep semantic information can assist in resolving some of these issues. Examples of table tennis balls falsely labeled as positive is shown Figure 23. Notably, the occurrence of these False Positive targets and trajectories is typically brief. Therefore, directly filtering out trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{u}$$\end{document}Tuwith shorter lengths can significantly reduce the number of such targets. Similar strategies have also been proposed in6and7. Results of Filtering Out Short Trajectories is shown in Table A3 of Appendix A.Fig. 23Some examples of table tennis balls falsely labeled as positive (marked with red boxes).

Some examples of table tennis balls falsely labeled as positive (marked with red boxes).

Model Parameters and Inference Speed: Our proposed method not only exhibits excellent performance but also boasts a significantly small number of parameters, totaling merely 0.8M. In contrast, the YOLOv8n model possesses a substantial parameter count of 3.2M. This is partly due to our simplified approach of utilizing the HOG operator as the image feature extractor, without designing a specialized feature extraction network for small targets. Another contributing factor is the nature of graph neural networks, where model parameters are reused across nodes and edges during inference, potentially leading to the neglect of challenging and infrequently occurring samples.

Regarding inference speed, we conducted tests on Side-Facing Professional athlete Competition videos in both 1080P and 720P resolutions. The testing environment consisted of a docker container equipped with a Teala T4 graphics card (Memory 16GB), CPU limitations ranging from 4–16 cores, and memory restrictions between 8192-32768MB. The running time for each component of our algorithm is summarized in Table 6.Table 6Tracking Performance under Different Views.Stage of the Algorithm1080P720PTime Cost (s/frame)fpsTime Cost (s/frame)fpsDetector0.342.940.0811.81Preprocess for Graph0.0813.180.0252.33Graph Inference0.128.070.0519.01Organize Result0.0335.340.0188.50Total0.571.760.175.96

Table 6Tracking Performance under Different Views.Stage of the Algorithm1080P720PTime Cost (s/frame)fpsTime Cost (s/frame)fpsDetector0.342.940.0811.81Preprocess for Graph0.0813.180.0252.33Graph Inference0.128.070.0519.01Organize Result0.0335.340.0188.50Total0.571.760.175.96

Tracking Performance under Different Views.

Overall, inference speed is not a strength of our approach. Under the same testing conditions, YOLOv8’s preprocess, inference, and postprocess stages averaged 1.8ms, 16.2ms, and 2.1ms, respectively, resulting in an average frame time of 20.1ms and an inference performance exceeding 45fps (49.7fps). Furthermore, the running time of our solution is sensitive to the resolution of the video images, as the time spent by the detector is directly proportional to the image resolution. Nevertheless, the current performance of our algorithm is sufficient to meet the needs of offline inference services for users. If we consider only the Graph Inference component, our model can achieve a performance of approximately 10-20fps (without utilizing half-precision acceleration for inference in our tests). With optimizations to the detector, such as employing separate processes or adopting alternative faster solutions, our approach has the potential to achieve real-time tracking and detection.

Feasibility of Widespread Application:We downloaded a brief tennis match video (consisting of 155 frames) from the Wimbledon Tennis Championships official website49. We brutally applied the model previously used for table tennis tracking and the same environment utilized for table tennis tactical analysis to detect tennis balls in the video, subsequently analyzing them in a similar manner. The analysis results presented in Table 7 demonstrate that even with a model, method, and technical solution specifically designed for table tennis, a certain level of effectiveness can be achieved in a tennis scenario (Examples of successfully detected tennis ball is shown in Figure 24). Based on this, we can boldly hypothesize that our proposed technical solution, with targeted modifications and training, can be applied to other ball sports such as tennis, badminton, football, basketball, volleyball, and so on. Additionally, the GMP model itself can be utilized for similar problems such as link discovery in graphs.Fig. 24The tennis ball successfully detected in a tennis match scenario.Table 7Analysis Results for Tennis Match Scenarios.PrecisionRecallF1-ScoreTennis Target0.8820.6060.719Tennis Relationship0.7860.4730.591 The definitions of “Target” and “Relationship” in the table are identical to those in table tennis. Conclusion and future works

In this paper, we addressed the challenging problem of tracking fast-moving table tennis balls, which are small-sized, rapidly moving, and have non-significant image features. To tackle this, we employed a combined approach of detection and discrimination. Specifically, for the discriminator, we proposed a novel graph neural network called GMP, which features a unique message passing mechanism tailored to address the target tracking problem. Our experimental results demonstrated that our algorithm performs well in various scenarios within the dataset and holds promise for application to other high-speed target tracking problems.

For the future works,we may improve some details in our method considering some problems and limitations. Firstly, consider the aspect of image features. We solely utilized simple HOG features. It is evident that the use of image features containing semantic information could further enhance performance. In terms of target detection, simple difference detection and connected component analysis may not work effectively in all scenarios and are susceptible to interference from athletes and video noise within the scene. Therefore, more accurate scene modeling and target detection algorithms could help improve the algorithm’s effectiveness. Further more, it would be a promising research topic to track high-speed targets such as table tennis balls using the state-of-the-art Segment Everything50 as a detector, while incorporating contextual information. Additionally, modifying and enhancing the performance of the algorithm proposed in this paper or directly applying it to other ball sports or tracking problems are viable research directions or tasks for future exploration.
